#define RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(IT, N)                                        \
  const r_ssize* v_dimensions = IT->v_dimensions;                              \
  const int dimensionality = IT->dimensionality;                               \
  const r_ssize* v_strides = IT->v_strides;                                    \
                                                                               \
  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {                          \
    r_ssize v_axis_strides[N];                                                 \
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < N; ++i) {                                          \
      v_axis_strides[i] = v_strides[axis * N + i];                             \
    }                                                                          \
    const r_ssize axis_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];                         \
                                                                               \
    ++IT->v_point[axis];                                                       \
                                                                               \
    if (IT->v_point[axis] < axis_dimension) {                                  \
      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < N; ++i) {                                        \
        IT->v_loc[i] += v_axis_strides[i];                                     \
      }                                                                        \
      break;                                                                   \
    }                                                                          \
                                                                               \
    IT->v_point[axis] = 0;                                                     \
                                                                               \
    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < N; ++i) {                                          \
      IT->v_loc[i] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * v_axis_strides[i];                \
    }                                                                          \
  }                                                                            \
                                                                               \
  IT->start = IT->end;                                                         \
  IT->end += v_dimensions[0];

static inline void rray_run_iterator_next1(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it, 1);
}

static inline void rray_run_iterator_next2(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it, 2);
}

static inline void rray_run_iterator_next3(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it, 3);
}

static inline void rray_run_iterator_next4(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(it, 4);
}

#undef RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N

338	// Builds a flat location into `x` from the current multidimensional point
339	// represented by the indices.
340	static inline r_ssize rray_index_location(
341	  const r_ssize* v_x_strides,
342	  const int* const* v_v_index,
343	  const r_ssize* v_location,
344	  const r_ssize* v_run_stride,
345	  r_ssize run_i,
346	  r_ssize indices_size
347	) {
348	  r_ssize out = 0;
349	
350	  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < indices_size; ++i) {
351	    const int* v_index = v_v_index[i];
352	    const r_ssize location = v_location[i] + run_i * v_run_stride[i];
353	    const int index = v_index[location];
354	    out += (r_ssize) (index - 1) * v_x_strides[i];
355	  }
356	
357	  return out;
358	}

branch 'feature/iterator-index' set up to track 'origin/feature/iterator-index'.
ac97cd8 Move rray_index() to the run iterator and add index benchmarks

rray_index() now uses the run iterator, and it's committed and pushed to feature/iterator-index as ac97cd8. With 1 to 4 coordinate arrays it's as fast as before or faster. With 5 or more it's about 10% slower, which you said is fine. All 3497 tests pass.

What changed

  • src/strided-iterator2.h: added next3() and next4(). All of next1() through next4() now use one small macro, RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(), which is #undefed right after. The generic next() is unchanged, as you asked.

  • src/index.c: each storage type builds its iterator itself. The switch on the number of coordinate arrays stays: cases 1 to 4 call next1() to next4(), and anything larger calls the generic next(). The strides along the first axis are copied once before the walk, and each index's location is copied once per run. rray_index_location() is the same as on main.

  • Removed code that nothing uses any more: the n plan in src/strided-iterator.h (rray_strided_iterator_n_plan, RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(), rray__strided_iterator_axes_coalesce_n()) and rray_fill_broadcast_strides_from_arrays(). Overall the change removes about 120 lines.

  • bench/index.R: 23 cases in the same form as bench/slice.R, timing rray_index() only. They cover 1 to 5 coordinate arrays, matched one to one and crossed, a single scalar coordinate, short runs, a leading axis of size 1, missing values, and every storage type.

Two things I found while benchmarking

  • Order of loads in the shared macro. My first version of the macro made next2() 11% slower when adding [1, 10, 1] to [10, 10, 10000]. The hand-written next2() read both strides before writing to v_point, and the macro read them after. All of these live in the same struct, so the compiler couldn't reorder them. The macro now copies the strides into a local array of size N first. That brought next2() back to baseline, and broadcast and sum also match baseline.

  • Copying locations into locals. When the loop read loc straight from the iterator struct, the compiler reloaded it and multiplied on every element. Copying it into locals once per run fixed that.

Results (2 runs × 200 iterations, old build vs new build, both at -O2)

Case Change
1D to 4D, all shapes same as before
Raw storage 33% faster
Complex storage about 30% faster
Character and list storage same or a little faster
Short runs ([10, 1e5] crossed product) about 5% slower on the fastest run, 1% to 2% on the median
5D fallback about 10% slower

The short-run cost comes from the iterator in #112 itself. Its start, end and v_loc stay in memory between runs, while the old code kept them in registers. plans/iterators.md already recorded the same 4% to 6% cost for addition.

Things I left alone

  • The "Next specialization" comment in src/strided-iterator2.h still mentions only next1() and next2(), and says most callers have one or two inputs. It's your comment, so I didn't edit it. You may want to mention next3() and next4().

  • bench/iterator.R stops partway through because it calls rray_sum_along(), which no longer exists.

311	#define RRAY_RUN_ITERATOR_NEXT_N(IT, N)                                        \
312	  const r_ssize* v_dimensions = IT->v_dimensions;                              \
313	  const int dimensionality = IT->dimensionality;                               \
314	  const r_ssize* v_strides = IT->v_strides;                                    \
315	                                                                               \
316	  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {                          \
317	    r_ssize v_axis_strides[N];                                                 \
318	    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < N; ++i) {                                          \
319	      v_axis_strides[i] = v_strides[axis * N + i];                             \
320	    }                                                                          \
321	    const r_ssize axis_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];                         \

+static inline void rray_run_iterator_next1(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  const r_ssize* v_dimensions = it->v_dimensions;
+  const int dimensionality = it->dimensionality;
+  const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides;
+
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const r_ssize axis_stride = v_strides[axis];
+    const r_ssize axis_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    ++it->v_point[axis];
+
+    if (it->v_point[axis] < axis_dimension) {
+      it->v_loc[0] += axis_stride;
+      break;
+    }
+
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+
+    it->v_loc[0] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride;
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end += v_dimensions[0];
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_next2(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  const r_ssize* v_dimensions = it->v_dimensions;
+  const int dimensionality = it->dimensionality;
+  const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides;
+
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const r_ssize* v_axis_strides = v_strides + axis * 2;
+    const r_ssize axis_stride1 = v_axis_strides[0];
+    const r_ssize axis_stride2 = v_axis_strides[1];
+    const r_ssize axis_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    ++it->v_point[axis];
+
+    if (it->v_point[axis] < axis_dimension) {
+      it->v_loc[0] += axis_stride1;
+      it->v_loc[1] += axis_stride2;
+      break;
+    }
+
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+
+    it->v_loc[0] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride1;
+    it->v_loc[1] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride2;
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end += v_dimensions[0];
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_next3(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  const r_ssize* v_dimensions = it->v_dimensions;
+  const int dimensionality = it->dimensionality;
+  const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides;
+
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const r_ssize* v_axis_strides = v_strides + axis * 3;
+    const r_ssize axis_stride1 = v_axis_strides[0];
+    const r_ssize axis_stride2 = v_axis_strides[1];
+    const r_ssize axis_stride3 = v_axis_strides[2];
+    const r_ssize axis_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    ++it->v_point[axis];
+
+    if (it->v_point[axis] < axis_dimension) {
+      it->v_loc[0] += axis_stride1;
+      it->v_loc[1] += axis_stride2;
+      it->v_loc[2] += axis_stride3;
+      break;
+    }
+
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+
+    it->v_loc[0] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride1;
+    it->v_loc[1] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride2;
+    it->v_loc[2] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride3;
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end += v_dimensions[0];
+}
+
+static inline void rray_run_iterator_next4(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
+  const r_ssize* v_dimensions = it->v_dimensions;
+  const int dimensionality = it->dimensionality;
+  const r_ssize* v_strides = it->v_strides;
+
+  for (int axis = 1; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    const r_ssize* v_axis_strides = v_strides + axis * 4;
+    const r_ssize axis_stride1 = v_axis_strides[0];
+    const r_ssize axis_stride2 = v_axis_strides[1];
+    const r_ssize axis_stride3 = v_axis_strides[2];
+    const r_ssize axis_stride4 = v_axis_strides[3];
+    const r_ssize axis_dimension = v_dimensions[axis];
+
+    ++it->v_point[axis];
+
+    if (it->v_point[axis] < axis_dimension) {
+      it->v_loc[0] += axis_stride1;
+      it->v_loc[1] += axis_stride2;
+      it->v_loc[2] += axis_stride3;
+      it->v_loc[3] += axis_stride4;
+      break;
+    }
+
+    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
+
+    it->v_loc[0] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride1;
+    it->v_loc[1] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride2;
+    it->v_loc[2] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride3;
+    it->v_loc[3] -= (axis_dimension - 1) * axis_stride4;
+  }
+
+  it->start = it->end;
+  it->end += v_dimensions[0];
+}

The macro is gone. next1() and next2() are back to exactly what's on main, and next3() and next4() are written by hand in the same pattern, with each stride in its own local. This is pushed to feature/iterator-index as 4e4851c.

All 3497 tests pass. The index benchmark looks the same as before this change:

  • 1 to 4 coordinate arrays: as fast as the old code. Raw storage is about 30% faster and complex storage about 25% faster.

  • Short runs (a 10-row matrix): about 3% slower on the fastest run and about 2% on the median. This is the cost of the iterator itself.

  • 5 or more coordinate arrays: about 11% slower, which you said was acceptable.

Davis

Rather than copying v_run_strides and v_locations out, do you think it would work to expose

rray_run_iterator_v_loc(it)
rray_run_iterator_v_axis_strides(it, axis)

And just use those directly? Or does that get in the way of compiler optimization?

Claude

I expect it would make the 1D to 4D paths slower. My first attempt did nearly exactly this, but that result is muddied, so it's worth a clean test.

What happened when I tried it. My first version had rray_index_location() read loc and stride straight from the iterator on every element, with no local copies. Against main it was:

  • 7% slower on the three-dimensional product

  • 11% slower on four dimensions with missing values

  • 8% slower on raw storage

  • Same speed on the five-dimensional fallback

The assembly for the raw path showed why. Each element reloaded loc and stride from the struct and did a mul. With local copies, the compiler instead keeps one pointer per index in a register and just adds the stride each step.

Why the compiler can't do that by itself. It can only keep a value in a register through the inner loop if it can prove that no write in the loop changes it. The loop writes to v_out, and the iterator's address escapes (rray_run_iterator() returns it through a pointer and isn't always inlined), so the compiler has to assume those writes might land in it. Raw output is unsigned char, which C allows to alias anything, so raw is guaranteed to hit this. Character and list storage also hit it, because their writes are calls to SET_STRING_ELT() and SET_VECTOR_ELT(), which the compiler can't see into. Copying into locals that never escape removes the question.

Why the test is muddied. That first attempt also used my next() macro, which I later found slowed next2() by 11% on its own. So some of those slowdowns, especially the integer cases, may have come from the macro and not from the direct reads. The raw slowdown is the part I'm confident about, since the assembly showed it directly.

What I'd expect from your proposal now:

  • rray_run_iterator_v_axis_strides(it, 0) for the strides is probably fine for integer and double output. It's read once, before the walk, and typed writes can't alias r_ssize reads. For raw, character and list output it likely brings back the per-element reload.

  • rray_run_iterator_v_loc(it) used directly in the inner loop has the same problem for those types. It would also speed the 5D fallback back up, since that path can't do the register trick anyway.

If you'd like, I can measure it cleanly now that next1() through next4() are hand-written again. It's a small change: add the two accessors and pass their pointers to rray_index_location(). That would tell us whether the simpler code is worth what it costs.

Davis

Measure it cleanly, I’m ok with small slowdowns if they aren’t crazy

Claude

The pointer version works, and its worst slowdown is about 10% on short runs. Overall it trades the copy version's raw-storage gain for a fixed 5D fallback. It's in the working tree, uncommitted, and the index tests pass.

Here are the three builds, using the fastest of 3 runs × 200 iterations. The medians agree except where I note it.

Case copy (committed) vs main pointer vs main
1D to 4D, most cases ±2% ±2%
2D product +1% +6% (median +3%)
Short runs ([10, 1e5]) +6% (median +1%) +18% (median +9%)
Leading unit axis +1% +26% on the fastest run, but the median is unchanged, so probably noise
3D with missing values 0% −10%
5D fallback +12% −1%
5D fallback with missing values +13% 0%
Raw storage −34% +7%
Complex storage −40% −36%
Character and list storage −3% to −6% −2% to −4%

So the pointer version:

  • Fixes the 5D fallback completely, because there's no copy per run.

  • Loses the raw-storage gain, as I predicted: raw writes can alias the iterator, so loc and stride get reloaded on every element. It ends up 7% slower than main rather than 34% faster.

  • Costs more on short runs, up to 9% on the median, because every element reads through the struct instead of from registers.

  • Keeps the complex, character and list gains.

The code is simpler. The per-run copy and the stride copy are both gone, and the only additions are two one-line accessors in src/strided-iterator2.h.

I'd take the pointer version. Nothing is more than about 10% slower than main, and the 5D regression is gone. The cost is the short-run case and the raw-storage gain, and raw is rarely used.

Should I commit and push the pointer version, or revert to the committed copy version?

Davis

Let’s commit and push it

Claude

8155694 Read index locations and strides through run iterator pointers

The pointer version is committed and pushed to feature/iterator-index as 8155694. All 3497 tests pass.

This commit adds two accessors to src/strided-iterator2.h, rray_run_iterator_v_loc() and rray_run_iterator_v_axis_strides(). In src/index.c, both pointers are fetched once before the walk, and the per-run and per-stride copies are gone.

Davis

Does the placement of this access matter or can it be after end inside the loop? Mimicking the idea of how other loops pull a single location and stride right there

Claude

I expect placement doesn't matter. Neither accessor reads memory: it.v_loc is just the iterator's address plus a fixed offset, and v_axis_strides(&it, 0) is v_strides + 0 * n, which the compiler reduces to the same kind of fixed address. The values are only read later, inside rray_index_location(). I'll check by compiling both placements and comparing the assembly.

Bash: cd ~/files/r/packages/rray4/src && S=/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-… 
cd ~/files/r/packages/rray4/src && S=/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/a94ecfd3-8444-44cb-97e7-93d53fd9d486/scratchpad && clang -arch arm64 -I/Library/Frameworks/R.framework/Versions/4.6/Resources/include -DNDEBUG -I./rlang -fPIC -O2 -S index.c -o $S/place-top.s && sed -n '/^#define RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(/,/^#define RRAY_INDEX_ATOMIC_POKE/p' index.c
#define RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(POKE, NEXT, INDICES_SIZE)                              \
  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); NEXT(&it)) {                            \
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);                        \
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);                            \
                                                                               \
    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                    \
      const r_ssize location = rray_index_location(                            \
        v_x_strides,                                                           \
        v_v_index,                                                             \
        v_location,                                                            \
        v_run_stride,                                                          \
        i - start,                                                             \
        INDICES_SIZE                                                           \
      );                                                                       \
      POKE(out, i, v_x[location]);                                             \
    }                                                                          \
  }

#define RRAY_INDEX_LOOP_MISSING(POKE, MISSING, NEXT, INDICES_SIZE)             \
  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); NEXT(&it)) {                            \
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);                        \
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);                            \
                                                                               \
    for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                    \
      const r_ssize location = rray_index_location_missing(                    \
        v_x_strides,                                                           \
        v_v_index,                                                             \
        v_location,                                                            \
        v_run_stride,                                                          \
        i - start,                                                             \
        INDICES_SIZE                                                           \
      );                                                                       \
      POKE(out, i, location == -1 ? MISSING : v_x[location]);                  \
    }                                                                          \
  }

#define RRAY_INDEX_ITERATE(POKE, MISSING)                                      \
  const r_ssize* v_location = rray_run_iterator_v_loc(&it);                    \
  const r_ssize* v_run_stride = rray_run_iterator_v_axis_strides(&it, 0);      \
                                                                               \
  if (any_missing) {                                                           \
    switch (indices_size) {                                                    \
    case 1:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP_MISSING(POKE, MISSING, rray_run_iterator_next1, 1);      \
      break;                                                                   \
    case 2:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP_MISSING(POKE, MISSING, rray_run_iterator_next2, 2);      \
      break;                                                                   \
    case 3:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP_MISSING(POKE, MISSING, rray_run_iterator_next3, 3);      \
      break;                                                                   \
    case 4:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP_MISSING(POKE, MISSING, rray_run_iterator_next4, 4);      \
      break;                                                                   \
    default:                                                                   \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP_MISSING(                                                 \
        POKE,                                                                  \
        MISSING,                                                               \
        rray_run_iterator_next,                                                \
        indices_size                                                           \
      );                                                                       \
      break;                                                                   \
    }                                                                          \
  } else {                                                                     \
    switch (indices_size) {                                                    \
    case 1:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(POKE, rray_run_iterator_next1, 1);                       \
      break;                                                                   \
    case 2:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(POKE, rray_run_iterator_next2, 2);                       \
      break;                                                                   \
    case 3:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(POKE, rray_run_iterator_next3, 3);                       \
      break;                                                                   \
    case 4:                                                                    \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(POKE, rray_run_iterator_next4, 4);                       \
      break;                                                                   \
    default:                                                                   \
      RRAY_INDEX_LOOP(POKE, rray_run_iterator_next, indices_size);             \
      break;                                                                   \
    }                                                                          \
  }

#define RRAY_INDEX_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE) v_out[I] = (VALUE)

function best(a,k,v,  i,m){m=1e18; for(i=1;i<=n[k,v];i++) if(a[k,v,i]+0<m) m=a[k,v,i]+0; return m}
function mid(a,k,v,  i,x,c,t,j){c=n[k,v]; for(i=1;i<=c;i++) x[i]=a[k,v,i]+0; for(i=1;i<=c;i++)for(j=i+1;j<=c;j++) if(x[j]<x[i]){t=x[i];x[i]=x[j];x[j]=t}; return x[int((c+1)/2)]}
END { for(i=1;i<=o;i++){k=order[i]; b=best(mins,k,"exp"); a=best(mins,k,"loop"); mb=mid(meds,k,"exp"); ma=mid(meds,k,"loop");

Placement doesn't affect speed. Putting the accessors inside each loop, after end, benchmarks the same as fetching them once before the switch.

Out of 23 cases, 20 are within ±3% on both the fastest run and the median. The other three are noise:

  • Complex: +20% on the fastest run but −57% on the median. That's GC noise.

  • Four-dimensional with missing values: −7% on the fastest run, +1% on the median.

  • Double: −5% to −6% on both.

The assembly isn't identical. The in-loop version is 119 instructions shorter, because the two pointers don't have to be kept alive across the whole switch. That doesn't show up in the timings.

So it can go where it reads best, and matching the other loops (fetch the location and stride right after start and end) is a good reason to put it inside. That version is in the working tree, uncommitted, and the index tests pass with it. Should I commit and push it?

Davis

In the meantime, did it seem important that we don’t pass a pointer to it in to rray index location?

Claude

Probably not, though I haven't measured it directly.

My first attempt did pass &it into rray_index_location(), which read rray_run_iterator_loc(it, i) and rray_run_iterator_stride(it, i) for each element. After inlining, that's the same loads from the same offsets in the struct as the current version, which passes v_location and v_run_stride pointers taken from it. Either way the compiler sees the same memory.

The numbers fit that:

  • Raw storage: +8% with &it, and +7% with the pointers. Raw is the case where the aliasing problem shows up, and it came out the same both ways.

  • 3D product (+7%) and 4D with missing values (+11%): these slowdowns appeared with &it but not with the pointers. That first attempt also used my next() macro, which I later measured at 11% slower on its own. So the macro, not &it, most likely explains those.

What actually mattered was whether values got copied into locals that never escape, not whether you pass &it or pointers into it. The header's warning is about passing the iterator to a function that isn't inlined. A static inline helper like rray_index_location() is fine, which is why the accessors take &it too.

If you'd like a firm answer, I can benchmark a version that passes &it against the current one. It's a small change, and the run takes about 10 minutes.
